NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}

1 Executive Summary

Product Introduction
Mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx is a folate receptor-alpha (FRα)- directed antibody-drug
conjugate (ADC). It contains a chimeric IgG1 directed against FRα, a cleavable linker, and a DM4
payload. DM4 is a microtubule-disrupting agent. This product is not approved for any indication
worldwide.
The Applicant proposed the following indication for BLA 761310:
[TRADENAME] is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with folate receptor-alpha (FRα)
positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer, who
have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens. Select patients for therapy based
on an FDA-approved test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and
duration of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification
and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial.
The recommended indication for accelerated approval is:
ELAHERE™ is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with folate receptor-alpha (FRα)
positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer, who
have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens. Select patients for therapy
based on an FDA-approved test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and
duration of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification
and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial.
The recommended dose of mirvetuximab soravtansine is 6 mg/kg adjusted ideal body weight
(AIBW) administered once every 3 weeks (21-day cycle) as an intravenous infusion until disease
progression or unacceptable toxicity.
The total dose of ELAHERE is calculated based on each patient’s AIBW using the following
formula:
AIBW = Ideal Body Weight (IBW [kg]) + 0.4*(Actual weight [kg] – IBW)
Female IBW (kg) = 0.9*height(cm) – 92
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

12

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review team recommends accelerated approval of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx for
treatment of patients with FRα-positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or
primary peritoneal cancer, who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens,
in accordance with 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) Part 601.41, Subpart E for Biological
Products Licensing Regulations. This recommendation is primarily based on efficacy and safety
data from Study 0417 (SORAYA, NCT04296890), a single arm trial in 106 patients with FRαpositive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who
had received one to three prior systemic regimens. To be eligible, patients had to have tumor
tissue with “high” expression for FRα, which was determined by the VENTANA FOLR1 (FOLR12.1) RxDx Assay. All patients were also required to and did receive prior bevacizumab. Patients
received mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx 6 mg/kg (based on AIBW) as an intravenous infusion
every 3 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Tumor response assessments
occurred every 6 weeks for the first 36 weeks and every 12 weeks thereafter.
The major efficacy outcome measures were confirmed overall response rate (ORR) as assessed
by investigator according to response evaluation criteria in solid tumors (RECIST) v1.1 and
duration of response (DOR). The efficacy evaluable population included 104 patients with
platinum-resistant disease, who had measurable disease, and received at least one dose of
ELAHERE. Confirmed ORR by investigator was 31.7% (95% CI: 22.9, 41.6). Median DOR was 6.9
months (95% CI: 5.6, 9.7), with a 4.8% complete response rate and a 26.9% partial response
rate. Response assessment results using independent radiology review were consistent with
investigator assessment.
Available therapies in this treatment setting are limited to single agent chemotherapies with
ORRs ranging from 6-27%. However, these data were collected prior to establishment of a
platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab followed by maintenance treatment with bevacizumab
and/or a poly(ADP-ribose) polymerase (PARP) inhibitor as standard of care for advanced
ovarian cancer. More contemporary data suggest available chemotherapies have ORRs of 1213% and median DORs of 4-7.4 months, and these data come from clinical trials where patients
were less heavily pre-treated than those patients enrolled to Study 0417. It is also notable that
the patient population enrolled into Study 0417 was a biomarker-defined population with FRαexpressing tumors. To date, there are no other available therapies for patients with ovarian
cancer which target this biomarker.
Mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx demonstrated acceptable tolerability for the indicated
population with a serious and life-threatening disease. The most common adverse reactions
(≥20 %) including laboratory abnormalities, were vision impairment, fatigue, increased
aspartate aminotransferase, nausea, increased alanine aminotransferase, keratopathy,
abdominal pain, decreased lymphocytes, peripheral neuropathy, diarrhea, decreased albumin,
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

13

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
constipation, increased alkaline phosphatase, dry eye, decreased magnesium, decreased
leukocytes, decreased neutrophils, and decreased hemoglobin. Serious adverse reactions
occurred in 31% of patients receiving mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx, including intestinal
obstruction (8%), ascites (4%), infection (3%), and pleural effusion (3%). Fatalities due to
adverse reactions occurred in 2% of patients including small intestinal obstruction (1%) and
pneumonitis (1%).
Ocular toxicity is an important safety signal related to mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx. Ocular
adverse reactions occurred in 61% of patients with ovarian cancer treated with mirvetuximab
soravtansine-gynx. Nine percent (9%) of patients experienced Grade 3 ocular adverse reactions,
including visual impairment, keratopathy/keratitis, dry eye, photophobia, and eye pain; and one
patient (0.2%) experienced Grade 4 keratopathy. The prescribing information recommends
premedication and use of lubricating and ophthalmic topical steroids eye drops during
treatment with mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx. The prescribing information also includes a
Boxed Warning to advise health professionals of the risk of ocular toxicity. FDA will issue a
postmarketing requirement to further characterize the incidence and severity of mirvetuximab
soravtansine-related ocular adverse events and evaluate additional risk mitigation strategies for
ocular adverse events.
There was limited dosage exploration for mirvetuximab soravtansine, and the product was
associated with a high frequency of ocular toxicity. A different dosing schedule may have a
more optimal benefit-risk profile. FDA will issue a dose-finding postmarketing requirement for
evaluation of alternate dosing schedules.
A Pre-Market Approval (PMA) Application (PMA P220006) was submitted to CDRH for the
Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR-2.1) RxDx Assay. The PMA will receive contemporaneous approval with
the current BLA as a companion diagnostic device to identify patients with platinum-resistant
epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer for treatment with mirvetuximab
soravtansine-gynx.
Study 0416 (MIRASOL) is an ongoing randomized Phase 3 trial comparing mirvetuximab
soravtansine to investigator’s choice chemotherapy in patients with FRα-positive, platinumresistant, epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who have received 1-3
prior lines of systemic therapy. FDA requested blinded topline interim ORR/DOR data from
Study 0416, . These ORR/DOR data were supportive of the results from Study 0417.
The results of this study will serve to confirm the benefit of mirvetuximab soravtansine for this
indication.
Overall, the benefit-risk profile based upon results from Study 0417 was favorable. Based on
review of this application, as well as the current therapeutic landscape, the review team
concludes that mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx represents a new treatment option for patients
with FRα-positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal
Version date: October 12, 2018

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
cancer, who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens. The ORR of 31.7%
with a median duration of 6.9 is a surrogate endpoint that is reasonably likely to predict clinical
benefit (i.e., improved survival or progression free survival) in patients with FRα-positive,
platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who had
received one to three prior systemic regimens cancer. This patient population has a significant
unmet medical need for better treatment options.
Therefore, the FDA review team recommends granting accelerated approval to mirvetuximab
soravtansine-gynx for the following indication:
ELAHERE is a folate receptor alpha (FRα)-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate
indicated for treatment of adult patients with FRα positive, platinum-resistant epithelial
ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cacner, who have received one to three prior
systemic treatment regimens. Select patients for therapy based upon an FDA-approved test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on tumor response rate and
duration of response. Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification
and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Ovarian cancer, including fallopian tube and primary peritoneal cancer, is the fifth leading cause of cancer-related death
for women in the United States. There will be an estimated 19,880 new cases of ovarian cancer and 12,810 deaths from
ovarian cancer in the U.S. in 2022. Unfortunately, there is no recommended screening for ovarian cancer for the general
population, and ovarian cancer is often diagnosed at an advanced stage. Five-year overall survival (OS) is only 49%.
Initial treatment for patients with advanced ovarian cancer is typically surgery and (neo)adjuvant chemotherapy with a
platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab. Patients who experience a complete response (CR) or partial response (PR) may
be candidates for maintenance treatment with bevacizumab and/or a PARP inhibitor. Although most advanced ovarian
cancer is initially platinum sensitive, virtually all patients ultimately develop platinum-resistant disease which is
associated with a limited overall survival and few treatment options.
Patients with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer who have received only 1-2 prior lines of systemic treatment and who
have not previously received bevacizumab may be candidates to receive bevacizumab in combination with paclitaxel,
topotecan, or pegylated liposomal doxorubicin (PLD). If patients cannot receive this regimen, or after progression of
disease on this regimen, treatment options are limited to single agent chemotherapy with paclitaxel, topotecan, PLD,
gemcitabine, or other options. Historical data suggest that single agent chemotherapies have ORRs ranging from 6-27%.
However, these data were collected prior to establishment of a platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab followed by
maintenance treatment with bevacizumab and/or a PARP inhibitor as standard of care for advanced ovarian cancer.
More contemporary data suggest available chemotherapies have ORRs of 12-13% and median DORs of 4-7.4 months. In
addition, a unique aspect of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx is that it targets FRα, and its activity is enhanced in tumors
with higher levels of FRα expression. To this end, there are currently no other available therapies for ovarian cancer
which target tumors overexpressing FRα.
The efficacy and safety assessment for mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx was primarily based on results from SORAYA, a
single-arm international trial which enrolled 106 patients with FRα-positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian,
fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer. Patients had received 1-3 prior lines of therapy and all patients had received
16
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
prior bevacizumab. Patients were selected for treatment if their tumors were positive for FRα expression according to
the Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR1-2.1) CDx assay. Patients received mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx 6 mg/kg intravenously
every 3 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Tumor response assessments occurred every 6 weeks
for the first 36 weeks and every 12 weeks thereafter. The primary endpoint was confirmed ORR assessed by investigator
according to RECIST v1.1. DOR was a secondary endpoint.
There were 104 patients in the efficacy evaluable population for SORAYA. Study 0417 enrolled 96% White patients, 2%
Asian patients, and 2% of patients with race not reported. Only 2% of patients were Hispanic or Latino. The patient
population was not representative of a U.S.-based population with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer, with respect to
race and ethnicity. Because of this, FDA issued a post-marketing commitment (PMC) for the Applicant to characterize the
safety and efficacy of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx in patients from racial and ethnic minority groups. Regarding
baseline disease characteristics and prior therapies, the Study 0417 population was representative of a U.S.-based
population with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer. All patients had high-grade serous cancer, and 51% of patients had
visceral metastases. Ten percent of patients had received 1 prior line of systemic therapy, 39% of patients had received 2
prior lines of systemic therapy, and 50% of patients had received 3 prior lines of systemic therapy. Thirty-eight percept of
patients had received prior treatment in the platinum-resistant setting. All patients had received prior bevacizumab, and
49% of patients had received a PARP inhibitor.
Confirmed ORR assessed by investigator according to RECIST v1.1 was 31.7% (95% CI: 22.9, 41.6) and median DOR was
6.9 months (95% CI: 5.6, 9.7). Confirmed ORR and DOR assessed by BICR were generally consistent with the results per
investigator. In addition, FDA requested blinded topline ORR/DOR data from Study 0416, an ongoing randomized trial in
a similar treatment setting to Study 0417. Study 0416 is fully enrolled. These ORR/DOR data were supportive of the
results from Study 0417.
The safety profile of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx is generally reflective of the maytansinoid DM4 payload, which is
an antimitotic agent that inhibits tubulin polymerization and microtubule assembly, resulting in cell cycle arrest and
apoptosis. The main target organs for mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx-associated toxicity include the eyes, lungs, and
peripheral nerves. Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 2% of patients of the 106 patients that made up the safety
population on Study 0417 and included pneumonitis and small intestinal obstruction. Serious adverse reactions occurred
in 31% of patients and included intestional obstruction (8%), ascites (4%), infection (3%), and pleural effusion (3%). The
most common adverse reactions (≥20%) included vision impairment, fatigue, transaminase elevation, keratopathy,
17
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
abdominal pain, decreased lymphocytes, peripheral neuropathy, diarreha, decreased albumin, constipation, increased
alkaline phosphatase, dry eye, decreased magnesium, decreased leukocytes, decreased neutrophils, and decreased
hemoglobin Adverse reactions leading to dose discontinuation occurred in 11% of patients, adverse reactions leading to
dose interruptions occurred in 39% of patients and adverse reactions leading to dos reduction occurred in 20% of
patients.
Ocular toxicity is an important safety signal associated with mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx and affected 61% of
patients enrolled to Study 0417, including 9% of patients experiencing grade 3 ocular adverse reactions and one patient
who experienced grade 4 keratopathy. There is a Boxed Warning for ocular toxicity in the mirvetuximab soravtansine
USPI to alert providers to the risk of developing severe ocular toxicities, including visual impairment, keratopathy, dry
eye, photophobia, eye pain, and uveitis. Patients should adhere to the premedication and required eye care plan
outlined in the USPI, and should be promptly referred to an eye care professional for any new or worsening ocular
symptoms.
Other important safety signals associated with mirvetuximab soravtansine include pneumonitis, peripheral neuropathey,
and risk of embryo-fetal toxicity, which are listed under Warnings and Precautions in the USPI.
Patients with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer, particularly those who have received prior bevacizumab, have limited
treatment options consisting of single agent chemotherapies. Contemporary data suggesting these therapies have ORRs
of 12-13% and median DORs of 4-7.4 months. Considering the limited therapeutic options for patients with platinumresistant ovarian cancer who have received bevacizumab, along with the results from Study 0417, and ORR/DOR data
from Study 0416, the review team considers that the efficacy of mirvetuximab soravtansine represents a meaningful
advantage compared to available therapies, and these results are reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit. Safety and
tolerability of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx were acceptable for a patient population with a serious and lifethreatening illness and limited treatment options. Adverse reactions are manageable through pretreatment, supportive
care, and labeling, including a Boxed Warning for ocular toxicity.
Overall, the benefit-risk assessment for mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx is favorable. There is a post-marketing
requirement (PMR) to submit results from the ongoing randomized trial, Study 0416, for confirmation of clinical benefit.
This trial is comparing mirvetuximab soravatansine to investigator’s choice chemotherapy in patients with FRα-positive,
platinum-resistant, epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who have received 1-3 prior lines of
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
systemic therapy.
Patients should be selected for treatment with mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx according to the Ventana FolR1 (FOLR2.1) RxDx Assay. The assay is an Immunohistochemistry (IHC) assay indicated to select patients with epithelial ovarian,
fallopian tube, or pirmaty peritoneal cancer whose tumor cells display membrane staining for the folate receptor alpha
(FOLR1) at moderate and/or strong intensity levels (≥75% viable tumor cells with membrane staining). The PMA
P220006 for the VENTANA FOLR1 (FOLR-2.1) RxDx Assay will be contemporaneously approved by CDRH with the BLA for
mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx.
Mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx is recommended for accelerated approval as follows:
Mirvetuximab soravtansine is a folate receptor alpha (FRα)-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate
indicated for the treatment of adult patients with FRα positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or
primary peritoneal cancer, who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens. Select patients for
therapy based on an FDA-approved test.

2 Therapeutic Context

Analysis of Condition
Ovarian cancer is the fifth leading cause of cancer-related death for women in the United
States. In 2022, there will be an estimated 19,880 new cases of ovarian cancer and 12,810
deaths from ovarian cancer. The most common type of ovarian cancer is epithelial ovarian
cancer, which comprises approximately 90% of all cases. Most epithelial ovarian cancers are
high-grade serous tumors which are associated with a poorer prognosis compared to other
ovarian cancer subtypes. There are no recommended screening measures for ovarian cancer for
the general population, and unfortunately, ovarian cancer is often detected at an advanced
stage. Ovarian cancer is associated with only a 49% five-year overall survival (American Cancer
Society 2022).
FRα is an isoform of the FR which is normally expressed in epithelial cells including the kidney,
retina, choroid plexus, and placenta. FRα may be expressed in >80% of serous ovarian cancers.
Data are conflicting regarding the association between FRα expression and tumor stage and
grade. Although it is hypothesized that FRα could mediate tumor progression, it is also unclear
if FRα expression is associated with prognosis for ovarian cancer. FRα expression appears to be
conserved with tumor progression (Vergote 2015).

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
Initial treatment for patients with advanced ovarian cancer is typically hysterectomy/bilateral
salphingo-oophorectomy with comprehensive staging and debulking. Patients then receive
adjuvant chemotherapy with a platinum-based doublet. Some patients with bulky disease who
are less likely to experience an optimal cytoreduction may receive neoadjuvant chemotherapy
first with a platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab, followed by an interval debulking surgery
with completion hysterectomy/BSO and cytoreduction, followed by adjuvant treatment with a
platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab (NCCN 2022). Patients who experience a CR or PR (7080% of patients) may be candidates to receive maintenance treatment with bevacizumab (if
received with platinum-based chemotherapy), niraparib, olaparib, or olaparib + bevacizumab
(Kemp 2013).
Most patients will experience recurrence of disease. Patients with platinum-sensitive disease
can receive treatment with a platinum-based doublet (± bevacizumab) and if they experience a
CR or PR, they may be candidates for maintenance treatment with bevacizumab (if received
with platinum-based chemotherapy), niraparib, olaparib, or rucaparib.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

24

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
Unfortunately, ultimately, almost all patients with advanced ovarian cancer develop platinumresistant disease, which has limited survival and few treatment options. Patients who have
received only 1-2 prior lines of systemic treatment and who have not previously received
bevacizumab may be candidates to receive bevacizumab in combination with chemotherapy,
either paclitaxel, topotecan, or pegylated liposomal doxorubicin (PLD) (AVASTIN USPI 2022). If
patients cannot receive this regimen or if patients experience progression of disease (PD) on
this regimen and are still candidates for systemic therapy, their treatment options are limited to
single agent chemotherapy with paclixel, topotecan, PLD, gemcitabine, or other options.
There are no therapies approved specifically for patients with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer
who have already received a bevacizumab-containing regimen. There are also no therapies
approved specifically for patients with FRα-positive ovarian cancer. Potential treatment options
for patients with platinum-resistant ovarian cancer who have already received a bevacizumabcontaining regimen are summarized in Table 2.

Reviewer comment: It is challenging to evaluate the efficacy data for available therapies. Trials
of the single agent chemotherapies occurred prior to adoption of the current treatment
paradigm for advanced ovarian cancer which includes a platinum-based doublet ± bevacizumab
followed by maintenance treatment with bevacizumab or a PARP inhibitor. The efficacy results
(ORR/DOR) in the table may not represent what patients would experience in the current era
(2022) of treatment for advanced ovarian cancer.
Data from the control arms for more recent trials is shown in Table 2 to get a sense of the
efficacy which could be expected with these single agent chemotherapy options in the current
era of treatment. It is important to note that even these trials are not reflective of the patients
enrolled to Study 0417. In AURELIA, patients only received 1-2 prior lines of treatment, and no
patients had received prior bevacizumab. In CORAIL, ~75% of patients had received 1-2 prior
lines of treatment, and ~40% of patients had received prior bevacizumab. In NINJA, ~60% of
patients had received 1-2 prior lines of treatment and it is unclear if patients had received prior
bevacizumab. In Study 0403(FORWARD 1), ~65% of patients had received 1-2 prior lines of
treatment, and ~50% of patients had received prior bevacizumab. In general, generation of the
efficacy results in the table occurred in less heavily pre-treated populatons than those patients
enrolled to Study 0417.

4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical
Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety

Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
DO1 consulted OSI to perform an audit of the Applicant as well as of the overall trial conduct.
FDA selected three sites (Site 026: Dr. Jason Konner, Site 504: Dr. Domenica Lorusso, Site 932:
Dr. Andrew Dean) for clinical inspection based on enrolling large numbers of patients in their
respective geographic regions and/or having a higher ORR than observed on the trial. The trial
sponsor, ImmunoGen. Inc., was also selected for inspection given that mirvetuximab
soravtansine is an NME, and there have been no prior inspections of this sponsor. Based on
these inspections, there were no significant regulatory violations, the trial conduct for Study
0417 appears adequate, and the submitted data from the three sites appear reliable.
Clinical inspection summaries are included here:

IMGN853-0417. This was the first FDA inspection of the investigator.

Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
A Pre-Market Approval (PMA) Application (PMA P220006) was submitted to CDRH for the
Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR-2.1) RxDx Assay.
The Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR-2.1) RxDx Assay is a qualitative immunohistochemistry assay that can
be used to assess folate receptor alpha (FOLR1) in formalin-fixed, paraffin-embedded epithelial
ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer tissue specimens by light microscopy. The
assay is for use with the OptiView DAB IHC Detection Kit for staining on a BenchMark ULTRA
instrument.
Effectiveness of the Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR-2.1) RxDx Assay was based upon pateints enrolled to
Study 0417. The eligibility for Study 0417 required that patients have tumors with positivite
FRα expression. FRα positivity was defined by the PS2+ scoring method, which is the
methodology in which the pathologist describes the percentage of tumor cells expressing a
certain intensity of membrane staining. The intensity of interest for the current study was
determined to be levels of moderate (2+) or strong (3+), and this was scored by assessing the
total number of viable tumor cells these staining levels. To be considered positive for FRα
expression, ≥ 75% of viable tumor cells in the specimen had to demonstrate FOLR1membrane
staining at moderate and/or strong staining intensity, as determined by the assay, which was
interpreted at a central site.
According to the CDRH team, the performance of the Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR1-2.1) RxDx Assay
was supported by the analytical performance validation studies. It was determined that the
data submitted in the PMA are supportive of approval of the Ventana FOLR1 (FOLR1-2.1) RxDx
Assay as a companion diagnostic deveic to identify patients with epithelial ovarian, fallopian

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology

Executive Summary
Mirvetuximab soravtansine (Elahere) is an antibody-drug conjugate (ADC) consisting of a
humanized immunoglobulin G1 antibody (M9346A) directed against folate receptor alpha
(FRα), a succinimidyl-4-(pyridine-2-yl) disulfanyl-2-sulfo-butyrate (sulfo-SPDB) linker, and
maytansine microtubule inhibitor, DM4. Each antibody is conjugated to an average of 3 to 4
molecules of DM4. Mirvetuximab soravtansine is administered by intravenous (IV) infusion
once every 3 weeks at the recommended dose of 6 mg/kg (based on AIBW) for the treatment of
adult patients with FRα positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary
peritoneal cancer, who have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens. The
nonclinical development program for mirvetuximab soravtansine and the cytotoxic payload,
DM4, consisted of studies in rats, monkeys, and rabbits to evaluate the primary and safety
pharmacology, general toxicity, ocular toxicity, phototoxicity, and genotoxicity. During the
nonclinical development, mirvetuximab soravtansine was referred to as IMGN853 or M9346Asulfo-SPDB-DM4.
Folate receptor alpha (FRα, also known as FOLR1 or FR1) is an N-glycosylated protein anchored
to the plasma membrane via glycosyl phosphatidylinositol with a molecular weight of 28 kD
(Shen, et al., 1994). FRα is upregulated on the surface of many epithelial-derived tumors,
compared to normal tissue. This receptor plays a critical role in cellular uptake of folate, a basic
component of cell metabolism and DNA synthesis and repair.
In primary pharmacology studies, mirvetuximab soravtansine bound to FRα expressed on
human or Rhesus monkey cells but not endogenous mouse or rat FRα. Binding to surface FRα
leads to internalization of the receptor-ADC complex and lysosomal degradation of
mirvetuximab soravtansine. This degradation releases the DM4 from the antibody and can
result in cell cycle arrest and cell death. In vivo, mirvetuximab soravtansine delayed tumor
growth and caused tumor regression in several ovarian cancer xenograft mouse models when
mice were administered a single IV dose of mirvetuximab soravtansine containing antibody
doses as low as 1.2 mg/kg and DM4 dose of 25 µg/kg. Based on the nonclinical pharmacology
studies provided in the BLA submission, the established pharmacological class (EPC) of “folate
receptor α-directed antibody and microtubule inhibitor conjugate” is appropriate based on its
pharmacological activity, prior EPC for related approved products, and the approved EPC for
microtubule inhibitors.
In vitro, DM4 was not stable in rat, human, and monkey plasma but was stable in human, rat,
and monkey blood for up to 90 minutes. Metabolite S-methyl-DM4 (DM4-Me) was stable in
human plasma either tested alone or in combination with DM4. In addition, S-methyl-DM4 was
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

36

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
stable in human, rat, and monkey blood after incubation for up to 90 minutes. The binding of
DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 to human plasma protein in vitro was 99.38% and 99.27%,
respectively.
In the 10-week GLP repeat-dose toxicity study in monkey, after a single IV dose of mirvetuximab
soravtansine, the plasma exposure (Cmax and AUC 0-24) increased in an approximately dose
proportional manner with no remarkable gender differences. The T1/2 values ranged from 95 to
106 hours on Day 1 and from 102 to 129 hours on Day 64. Upon repeated IV infusion of
mirvetuximab soravtansine once every 3 weeks, no accumulation was observed. All monkeys
administered mirvetuximab soravtansine at doses up to 8 mg/kg/dose tested negative for the
presence of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs). Animal to human exposure margins were calculated
using the mean clinical AUClast (15.91 h*mg/mL) for mirvetuximab soravtansine, Cmax (143900
ng/mL) for mirvetuximab soravtansine, and Cmax (4080 ng/mL) and AUClast (208.5 h*ng/mL) for
DM4 after the first dose in patients receiving the recommended dose. Per the clinical
pharmacology team, there was no accumulation with repeated dosing of mirvetuximab
soravtansine; therefore, the steady state exposures after repeated dosing were similar to
exposures seen after the first dose.
In safety pharmacology studies, inhibition of hERG channels was not evaluated with
mirvetuximab soravtansine; however, up to 60 µM DM4 and metabolite S-methyl-DM4 had <
50% inhibition on hERG potassium channels. Additionally, mirvetuximab soravtansine did not
have effects on the nervous, cardiovascular, or respiratory systems in monkeys after repeated
IV dosing up to 8 mg/kg (4-fold the exposures at the recommended dose based on AUC) based
on ECG analysis and clinical observation.
Following a single IV dose of 5 mg/kg radio-labeled mirvetuximab soravtansine to male Sprague
Dawley rats, high radioactivity concentrations were observed in liver, bone marrow, lung,
kidneys, and spleen. The eye:plasma ratio increased through the duration of the study,
suggesting that drug clearance from the eyes was slower than from plasma. Radioactivity was
also observed in testes suggesting that drug also penetrated the blood:testes barrier. The
brain:plasma ratio was < 3% of the plasma radioactivity of mirvetuximab soravtansine,
indicating limited blood:brain barrier penetration. The major route of mirvetuximab
soravtansine elimination was via feces in rats.
A 10-week GLP compliant repeat-dose toxicity study with mirvetuximab soravtansine was
conducted in cynomolgus monkey using the clinical IV route of administration, and the payload
DM4 was assessed in cynomolgus monkey in a non-GLP single IV dose toxicity study.
Administration of mirvetuximab soravtansine at 8 mg/kg for 10 weeks resulted in bone marrow
toxicity with corresponding decreases in absolute neutrophils, white blood cells, and
reticulocytes in both sexes when compared to control group. In addition, administration of
mirvetuximab soravtansine at dose ≥ 4 mg/kg resulted in ocular toxicity (minimal to marked
degeneration/necrosis and attenuation in the corneal epithelium and minimal to slight
epithelial degeneration/necrosis and inflammation of skin on the eyelids). Generally, the

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

37

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
hematopoietic and ocular toxicity fully or partially recovered by the end of the 5-week recovery
period. In humans, mirvetuximab soravtansine caused hematological toxicities, including
pancytopenia (≥ 10% Grade 2-4 decreased lymphocytes, hemoglobin, and neutrophils) at the
recommended dose of 6 mg/kg IV once every 3 weeks. Mirvetuximab soravtansine also caused
eye disorders (vision blurred, keratopathy, dry eye, cataract, photophobia, and eye pain) at the
recommended dose. Thus, toxicological findings in the hematopoietic system and eyes in
cynomolgus monkeys were consistent with the frequent adverse events observed in clinical
trials.
Mirvetuximab soravtansine at 5 to 10 mg/kg as a single dose and 8 mg/kg once every 3 weeks
for 10 weeks resulted in skin toxicity, including slight to moderate epithelial ulceration/erosion,
acanthosis, hyperkeratosis, and edema in both sexes (≥ 1-fold the exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC). Changes in skin were not observed at end of recovery
period. In addition, DM4 showed phototoxicity with IC50 values of 0.582 µg/mL and 8.712
µg/mL in the presence and absence of UV light, respectively, in an in vitro Balb/c 3T3 mouse
fibroblasts study. Mirvetuximab soravtansine showed positive staining of human skin in a tissue
cross reactivity study; however, skin toxicity was not observed in clinical trials.
Due to ocular toxicities observed in both animal toxicity studies and in patients, the Applicant
also conducted GLP compliant repeated-dose toxicity studies to evaluate ocular toxicity of
mirvetuximab soravtansine in male rabbits. Rabbits received up to 12 mg/kg/dose
mirvetuximab soravtansine IV once weekly for 4 weeks or up to 10 mg/kg/dose once every 3
weeks for 12 weeks. A dose of 12 mg/kg once weekly resulted in early moribund sacrifice on
Day 22 due to weight loss, anorexia, and skin lesions. Doses ≥ 4 mg/kg weekly caused
microscopic findings of attenuation and degeneration/necrosis of the corneal epithelium in the
eyes of rabbits. These findings were still observed at the end of a 3-week recovery period.
Similar microscopic findings in the eyes and skin were also observed in rabbits administered ≥ 4
mg/kg mirvetuximab soravtansine once every 3 weeks; however, these findings were less
severe when compared to once weekly dosing. Changes were still present following a 3-week
recovery period but trending towards recovery.
In a non-GLP single dose toxicity study of DM4, target organs of toxicity in cynomolgus monkeys
included bone marrow (depletion), lung (hemorrhage) and inflammation at the site of injection.
Microscopic findings in eye included mild mononuclear cell infiltration in ciliary body and
minimal mononuclear cell infiltration in uvea. Similar histopathology findings in bone marrow
and injection site were also observed in cynomolgus monkeys administered mirvetuximab
soravtansine for 10-weeks.
The Applicant submitted genotoxicity studies that evaluated the payload DM4 and its
metabolite S-methyl-DM4. DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 were negative in the vitro bacterial reverse
mutation assay but positive in the in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay. The genetic
toxicity results with DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 are consistent with the mechanism of action and
primary pharmacological activity. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

38

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
mirvetuximab soravtansine in accordance with ICH S9 guidance for advanced cancer
indications.
Dedicated reproductive toxicity studies were not conducted with mirvetuximab soravtansine in
accordance with ICH S9 based on the expected adverse effects of DM4 on actively dividing cells
and the intended indication for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer. Since
mirvetuximab soravtansine targets actively dividing cells (e.g., adverse effects on the
hematopoietic system in nonclinical studies and clinical trials) and is genotoxic, it is expected to
cause teratogenicity and/or embryo-fetal death if administered to a pregnant woman. In
addition, FRα gene mutations have been associated with human birth defects, such as neural
tube defects and neurologic disease (Trembath, et al., 1999; Cario, et al., 2009; and Steinfield,
et al., 2009). Effective contraception use is recommended for females of reproductive potential
during treatment with mirvetuximab soravtansine and for 7 months following the last dose.
Since mirvetuximab soravtansine is clastogenic, a contraception period of 6 months with an
additional 5 half-lives (mirvetuximab soravtansine half-life ~5 days) is recommended to
minimize the risk for DNA damage in oocytes, in accordance with FDA guidance Oncology
Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations. Additionally,
effective contraception use is recommended for male patients with female partners of
reproductive potential during treatment and for 4 months after the last dose (3 months with an
additional 5 half-lives) based on the FDA guidance. Due to the current ovarian cancer indication,
the Applicant and review team agreed not to include a contraception recommendation for
males in the prescribing information. In addition, mirvetuximab soravtansine has the potential
to cause adverse reactions in a breastfed child. Based on the plasma half-life of ~5 days in
patients, the majority of mirvetuximab soravtansine should be cleared after 25 days; thus,
women are advised not to breastfeed during treatment and for 1 month after the last dose.
Recommendation
The submitted nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology data with mirvetuximab soravtansine
are adequate to support approval of Elahere for the proposed indication.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
None.

Pharmacology
Primary pharmacology
Results of primary pharmacology of mirvetuximab soravtansine (IMGN853) were reviewed
primarily under IND 111915 and additional relevant pharmacology studies are also reviewed
and summarized below.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

39

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
Mirvetuximab soravtansine bound folate receptor 1 (FR1 or FRα) expressed on human (Kd =
0.05-0.1 nM) or Rhesus monkey cells (Kd = 0.1 nM) but not endogenous mouse or rat folate
receptor when determined by flow cytometry analysis (Study no. IMV28-004). When analyzed
by an ELISA method, mirvetuximab soravtansine bound to FRα expressed on human and
cynomolgus monkey with a similar affinity (Kd = 0.2 nM) (Study no. IMV28-006). Amino acid
sequence comparison showed high similarity (98%) between human FRα and Rhesus monkey
FRα. Rabbit, dog, mouse, and rat sequences showed 87-90% sequence similarity and 83-88%
sequence identity (Study no. IMV28-004).
The binding specificity of unconjugated antibody M9346A was analyzed by flow cytometry in
murine cells transfected with human FRα and compared with non-transfected murine cells. The
results showed that M9346A bound to human FRα expressing cells but not to non-transfected
cells. Similarly, M9346A did not bind to a FRα negative human multiple myeloma cell line (Study
no. IMV28-001). When analyzed by flow cytometry , M9346A and mirvetuximab soravtansine
also bound to endogenous FRα-positive human SKOV-3 ovarian cancer cells with the same Kd
value at 0.1 nM (Study no. IMV28-001). In addition, the binding affinity of M9346A and
mirvetuximab soravtansine was assessed by ELISA where recombinant human FRα-Fc protein
(human FRα fused to Fc moiety of murine IgG2a) was used as the antigen. The affinity value
(Kd) was 0.05 nM for both M9346A and IMGN853 (Study no. IMV28-001). M9346A also showed
no competitive binding with natural folate for binding to FRα (Study no. IMV28-001). Using an
ELISA assay, M9346A did not bind to human FRβ, FRɣ, or FRδ recombinant protein (Study no.
IMV28-010, Study IMV28-012).
To further assess the mechanism of action, the Applicant evaluated catabolism and lysosomal
processing of IMGN853 and unconjugated antibody M9346A (Study no. IMV28-008). In vitro,
when KB cervical carcinoma cells expressing high levels of FRα were incubated with 3HIMGN853 (M9346A-sulfo-SPDB-[3H]-DM4) for 22 hours, the major metabolites were lysine-N6sulfo-SPDB-DM4, DM4, and S-methyl-DM4. The metabolites represented approximately 12% of
the initial dose of 3H-DM4. To evaluate whether IMGN853 is activated primarily through
lysosomal degradation, the Applicant compared the rate for processing of 3H-IMGN853 to its
three catabolites to the catabolism rate of unconjugated 3H-M9346A antibody. M9346A was
labeled with the radioactive tracer, N-succinimidyl-[2,3, 3H] propionate to allow tracking of the
intact antibody and release of the free label after culture with KB cells for 22 hours. The amount
of protein-free radioactivity (processed) and protein-associated radioactivity (un-processed)
was assessed following acetone extraction and liquid scintillation counting. The percentage of
processed 3H-M9346A antibody and 3H-IMGN853 was similar, 28% vs. 23%, respectively. The
Applicant concluded that activation of IMGN853 was primarily through lysosomal degradation.
Thus, binding to surface FRα on cancer cell lines leads to internalization of the receptor-ADC
complex, lysosomal degradation of IMGN853, and intracellular release of maytansinoids.
Anti-tumor activity of IMGN853 was evaluated in several ovarian cancer xenograft mouse
models OVCAR-3, Igrov-1, and OV-90 (Study no. IMP28-001, IMP28-002, and IMP28-003).
Treatment with a single IV dose of IMGN853 resulted in either delays in tumor growth (21 days

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

40

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
to 120 days compared to control) or partial or complete regression with % tumor growth
inhibition ranging from 9%-78% compared to control group. Anti-tumor activity was noted with
IMGN853 containing antibody doses as low as 1.2 mg/kg and DM4 dose of 25 µg/kg (Study no.
IMP28-001, IMP28-002, IMP28-003).
IMGN853 mediated antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity in the presence of natural killer
cells (Study no. IMV28-002) but did not mediate complement dependent cytotoxicity (Study no.
IMV28-005). Moreover, antibody-dependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP) activity of IMGN853
was also evaluated by incubating human peripheral blood mononuclear cell (PBMC)-derived
macrophages with 2 target tumor cell lines expressing FRα on their cell surface
(adenocarcinoma KB cell line and human ovarian carcinoma IGROV1 cell line) in the presence of
either IMGN853 or M9346A antibody at various concentrations (6.67 x 10-8 M to 6.67 x 10-13 M)
and incubated for 2 hours at 37 ̊C in 5% CO2. The results demonstrated that IMGN853 and
M9346A antibody induced a concentration-dependent ADCP activity against 2 cell lines tested,
suggesting that conjugation to DM4 did not impact the ADCP activity of the antibody. The EC50
values were only determined for M9346A antibody, which were 1.4 x 10-11 M and 4.2 x10-11 M
ADCP activity for IGROV1 and KB cells, respectively.
IMGN853 bound to both human and cynomolgus monkey tissues by immunohistochemical
analysis (Study no. IMH28-003). Significant positive membrane staining was observed in 17
unique structural components isolated from 12 human tissues (lung, kidney, breast, choroid
plexus, fallopian tube, pancreas, parathyroid, pituitary, salivary gland, skin, spinal cord, and
stomach). Nine monkey tissues also showed significant positive staining in the choroid plexus,
fallopian tube, kidney, pancreas, salivary gland, parathyroid, pituitary, spinal cord but weak
staining in the skin, lung, breast, and stomach. The similarity of binding in human and
cynomolgus monkey tissues confirmed that the cynomolgus monkey was an appropriate model
for toxicological evaluation (Study no. IMH28-003).
Safety Pharmacology
Study Title/Number: Effects of DM4 and DM4-Me on cloned hERG potassium channels
expressed in human embryonic kidney cells/ 150328.JNO
Methods: The effect of DM4 (10-60 µM) and S-methyl-DM4 (DM4-Me, 10-60 µM) on in vitro
hERG current was evaluated by using stably transfected HEK293 cells and the whole cell patch
clamp method.
Results: The IC50 for the inhibitory effect of DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 on hERG potassium current
was not calculated but was estimated to be greater than 60 µM. Therefore, DM4 and S-methylDM4 are considered low potency potassium channel blockers.

plasma protein binding and

Plasma protein binding in rat,
monkey, and human

-The brain:plasma ratio indicated less than 3% of
the plasma radioactivity was in the brain.
-Fecal excretion was the observed predominant
route of elimination, accounting for a mean of
62.3% of the administered dose through 336
hours post-dose. Urinary excretion accounted for
a mean of 2.27% of elimination.
-DM4 was not stable in human, rat, and monkey
plasma. However, S-methyl-DM4 (DM4-Me) was
stable in human plasma either tested alone or in
combination with DM4 for up to 8 hours.
-DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 were stable in human,
rat, and monkey blood following incubation for up
to 90 minutes.
-The plasma protein binding of DM4 was 96-99%
across species.
-The blood-to-plasma partitioning of DM4 and Smethyl-DM4 was determined for human, rat, and
monkey following incubation for 60 minutes.
Overall, the blood-to-plasma concentration ratio
and percent associated with blood cells decreased
with increased DM4 concentrations.
-For S-methyl-DM4, partitioning to blood cells
decreased with increased S-methyl-DM4
concentrations in rat and monkey blood, but not
in human blood.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
GLP compliant single and repeated dose toxicity studies of IMGN853 and a non-GLP single dose
toxicity study of DM4 in cynomolgus monkeys were reviewed previously under IND 111915 and
summarized below.
Study title/ number: 10-week intravenous (slow push) toxicity and toxicokinetic study in
cynomolgus monkeys with a 5-week recovery period/ CVT28-001
IMGN853 at doses of 0, 2, 4, and 8 mg/kg/dose was administered intravenously (slow push)
to cynomolgus monkeys once every 3 weeks for up to 10 weeks (a total of 4 doses) with a 5week recovery period (n=6/sex/group). No early mortalities occurred. Drug-related clinical
signs of toxicity included broken skin, dry skin, discolored skin, scaly skin, sores, scabs,
and/or scars noted mostly in monkeys given ≥ 4 mg/kg/dose.
IMGN853-related ophthalmoscopic findings included keratopathy (faint perilimbal corneal
pigment migration into cornea, especially superiorly) of both eyes in 3/6 males and 3/6
females at 8 mg/kg/dose in Week 9. Corneal pigmentation was likely secondary to other
corneal changes. At the end of recovery period, similar corneal lesions developed in the
superior perilimbal cornea in one previously unaffected female at 8 mg/kg/dose. In
addition, the lesions (corneal pigmentation) were unchanged in a second female and more
advanced in both eyes in a male at 8 mg/kg/dose. Target organs of toxicity associated with
IMGN853 in monkeys were hematopoietic system (decreases ≥ 30% in absolute neutrophils,
white blood cell, and reticulocytes when compared to control in both sexes at 8 mg/kg
dose), injection sites (at 8 mg/kg/dose showed slight to moderate epithelial
ulceration/erosion); skin/subcutis (areas other than injection sites in males at 8 mg/kg/dose
showed slight to moderate epithelial ulceration/erosion, acanthosis, hyperkeratosis, and
edema); cornea (at ≥ 4 mg/kg/dose showed minimal to marked degeneration/necrosis,
pigmentation, and attenuation in the corneal epithelium); and skin of the eyelids (at ≥ 4
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

53

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
mg/kg/dose showed minimal to slight epithelial degeneration/necrosis and inflammation).
Hematology and histopathology findings were trending towards recovery at end of 5-week
recovery period. Histopathology findings of major target organs of toxicity for male and
female rats are presented in Table 4 and Table 5, respectively.
However, there were histopathology findings observed during the recovery period only,
including minimal axon degeneration in the sciatic nerve in 1/2 females at 8 mg/kg/dose
and minimal to slight fibrosis observed in testis in males at ≥ 2 mg/kg/dose IMGN853 in 1/2
males in each dose group.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
Study title/number: A single-dose intravenous (slow push) toxicity study of IMGN853
(M9346A-sulfo-SPDB-DM4) in cynomolgus monkeys/ WRT28-001
Five cynomolgus monkeys/sex/group were administered a single dose of IMGN853 at 0, 1,
3, 5, or 10 mg/kg. On Day 5, 3 animals/sex/group were sacrificed. The remaining recovery
group animals were sacrificed on Day 29. There were no unscheduled mortalities in this
study. The skin was a major target organ based on clinical observations of reddened areas
and desquamation and histopathology findings of moderate vasculitis and ulceration. These
toxicities occurred before Day 14 and with greater frequency in animals in the 10 mg/kg
dose group; however, they were also observed at the lowest dose of 1 mg/kg. The other
target organs of toxicity included injection site (minimal to moderate inflammation,
ulceration, edema, and hemorrhage), bone marrow (minimal to moderate bone marrow
depletion), spleen (mild lymphoid depletion), lymph nodes (mild lymphoid depletion),
kidney (minimal to mild mononuclear cell infiltration and calculus formation, along with
mild changes in clinical chemistry including decreased urea nitrogen and phosphorus at the
10 mg/kg dose), and lung (mild pleural fibrosis in the lung). Histopathology findings in bone
marrow, kidney, and lungs were still observed at end of recovery period with less severity.
There were no positive findings for anti-IMGN853 or anti-DM4 antibody production in any
animals.
Study title/number: Single dose toxicity study of maytansinoid DM4 in cynomolgus
monkeys/ CRT14-001
Monkeys were IV administered DM4 (L-DM4) at a dose of 0.1 mg/kg (n = 2/sex/ group) on
Day 1 and euthanized on Day 5 (1/sex/group) and Day 29 (1/sex/group). No eye
examination was performed/reported. All animals survived to the end of the study. Target
organs of DM4 in monkeys include bone marrow (moderate to severe erythroid and
myeloid depletion), lung (mild alveolar hemorrhage, erythrophagocytosis, and pigment),
and inflammation in several organs (minimal to mild mononuclear cell infiltration in
stomach, esophagus, and site of injection). Microscopic findings in eye included minimal
and mild mononuclear cell infiltrates in ciliary body and minimal mononuclear cell
infiltration in uvea.

In Vivo Clastogenicity Assay in Rodent (Micronucleus Assay)
Study title/ number: DM4: in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay/ CVT14-003
Key Study Findings:
• DM4 was clastogenic in male rats at 0.04 mg/kg.
GLP compliance: Yes
Test system: Rat bone marrow from animals administered DM4 intravenously twice
(approximately 24 hours apart) at 0.01, 0.02, and 0.04 mg/kg. The positive control was
cyclophosphamide and administered by oral gavage at 60 mg/kg/day.
Study is valid: Yes
Results
• DM4 induced statistically significant increases in the mean percent of micronucleated
polychromatic erythrocytes (PCE) in the high dose group when compared to the vehicle
control. The increases in micronucleated PCEs were outside of both the historical vehicle
control range and the 95% confidence interval.

(Excerpted from Applicant’s submission)
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

57

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
Study title/ number: S-methyl-DM4: in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay/ CVT14-004
Key Study Findings:
• S-methyl-DM4 was clastogenic in male rats at 0.02 and 0.04 mg/kg.
GLP compliance: Yes
Test system: Rat bone marrow from animals administered S-methyl-DM4 intravenously twice
(approximately 24 hours apart) at 0.01, 0.02, and 0.04 mg/kg. The positive control was
cyclophosphamide and administered by oral gavage at 60 mg/kg/day.
Study is valid: Yes
Results
• S-methyl-DM4 induced a dose-dependent and statistically significant increase in the
mean percent of micronucleated PCEs in the mid and high dose groups, compared to
the vehicle control. The increase in micronucleated PCEs in the high dose group were
outside the historical vehicle control range and the 95% confidence interval. In addition,
S-methyl-DM4 was cytotoxic to the bone marrow at 0.04 mg/kg/day.

(Excerpted from Applicant’s submission)
Carcinogenicity
Carcinogenicity studies were not needed to support the advanced cancer indication, consistent
with ICH S9 guidance.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
No reproductive or developmental animal toxicity studies were conducted with mirvetuximab
soravtansine. The cytotoxic component of ELAHERE, DM4, disrupts microtubule function, is
genotoxic, and can be toxic to actively dividing cells, suggesting it has the potential to cause
embryotoxicity and teratogenicity.

Phototoxicity:
Study Title/Number: DM4: in vitro Balb/c 3T3 neutral red uptake phototoxicity assay/CVT14005
Methods: The objective of this GLP-compliant study was to evaluate the phototoxicity potential
of DM4 (0.0316-100 µg/mL) as measured by the relative reduction in viability of Balb/c 3T3
mouse fibroblasts exposed to DM4 and ultraviolet radiation (+UVR), as compared with the
viability of fibroblasts exposed to DM4 in the absence of ultraviolet radiation (-UVR).
Chlorpromazine (0.01-100 µg/mL) was used as the positive control. Chlorpromazine cytotoxicity
and phototoxicity criteria were met, indicating that the assay was valid.
Results: Per OECD guideline, DM4 has phototoxicity potential under the conditions tested.

{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
Mirvetuximab soravtansine (ELAHERE) is an ADC consisting of a anti-FRα monoclonal antibody
(immunoglobulin G1 [IgG1]) attached via a cleavable linker to the cytotoxic maytansinoid
payload, DM4. Upon release from the antibody, DM4 inhibits tubulin polymerization and
microtubule assembly, resulting in cell cycle arrest and apoptosis. The proposed indication for
mirvetuximab soravtansine is the treatment of adult patients with folate receptor-alpha (FRα)
positive, platinum-resistant epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer, who
have received one to three prior systemic treatment regimens.
In the pivotal phase 3 trial, the ORR INV per RECIST v1.1 was 31.7% (95% CI: 22.9, 41.6). Median
DOR was 6.9 months (95% CI: 5.6, 9.7). Results for ORR and DOR as assessed by BICR were
generally consistent with results as assessed by INV, with an overall concordance of 86% for
ORR assessment between INV and BICR.
A major focus of this review was the appropriateness of the proposed dosage of 6 mg/kg
adjusted ideal body weight (AIBW) administered every 3 weeks (Q3W). Dosages ranging from
0.15‐7 mg/kg total body weight and 5 to 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W were investigated in the dose
escalation study. The recommended dose (i.e., 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W) was identified as the
maximum tolerated dose (MTD) and recommended phase 2 dose (RP2D) in the dose finding
trial. The major safety concern for this drug is ocular toxicity. The rate of ocular AEs in patients
treated with mirvetuximab soravtansine was 59%, including blurred vision (46%) and
keratopathy (37%). In addition, 35% of patients did not have resolution of their ocular AEs at
the last follow-up.
Exposure-response relationship for efficacy (ORR) and safety (ocular AEs of Grade 2+ and
peripheral neuropathy Grade 2+) were observed at the proposed 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W dosage.
Exposure‐efficacy analysis at the 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W dose level indicated that over the range of
exposures achieved at this dose level, lower exposure (AUC) was correlated with lower ORR.
However, there was no relationship between maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) of
mirvetuximab soravtansine and ORR. Additionally, there was a correlation between Cmax and
incidence of Grade2+ ocular toxicities. There was insufficient evaluation of the alternative
dosing schedules (e.g., modified weekly dosing regimen) that maintains the overall exposure
(ie., lowering Cmax, which is correlated with ocular toxicities while maintaining the overall
exposure, or AUC, that is associated with efficacy). Therefore, a dose finding PMR will be issued
for the following reasons:
- Dose exploration study was not robust and did not provide sufficient evidence that the
optimal dosage has been identified to maximize the risk-benefit profile.
- Mirvetuximab soravtansine caused very high rate of ocular toxicity

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

68

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
The review team also evaluated the dosing recommendations for patients with hepatic
impairment (HI). The clinical data contained limited to no information on patients with
moderate or severe HI. Population PK analysis indicated that patients with mild HI had similar
exposures of DM4 and its metabolite, s-methyl DM4 compared to patients with normal hepatic
function. Further, based on the available safety data, there was no apparent impact of hepatic
impairment on the safety including ocular AEs at the recommended dose of mirvetuximab
soravtansine. Therefore, no dose-modification may be necessary for patients with mild HI. With
regards to moderate or severe HI, unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4 exposures are
expected to increase in these patients. Additionally, liver metastasis is commonly reported in
patients with ovarian cancer. Therefore, avoiding use of mirvetuximab soravtansine in patients
with moderate or severe HI would deprive patients with poor baseline liver function (e.g.,
moderate HI). Therefore, a PMR will be issued to evaluate an appropriate starting dose of
mirvetuximab soravtansine in patients with moderate hepatic impairment.

Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology recommends the approval of BLA 761208 from a clinical
pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations/comments
are summarized below:

Recommendations and Comments
Primary evidence of the effectiveness of mirvetuximab
soravtansine in the proposed indication is derived from Study
0417. The observed ORR by IRC (primary endpoint) was 32.4%
(95% CI: 23.6- 42.2%). BORs included 5 patients (4.8%) with CR
and 29 patients (27.6%) with PR. The median DOR was 5.9
months (95% CI: 5.6, 7.7 months) by IRC.
The exposure-response analysis identified a correlation between
exposure and probability of achieving ORR. The analysis was
conducted with data primarily from one dose level and thus
needs to be interpreted with caution.
The proposed recommended dosage of mirvetuximab
soravtansine is 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W administered as an IV
infusion.
• No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild
HI (NCI-ODWG criteria). Mirvetuximab soravtansine should be
avoided in patients with moderate or severe HI.
• No dosage modification is recommended for patients with mild
to moderate renal impairment (CLcr ≥ 30 mL/min). There are
no data in patients with severe renal impairment (CLcr < 30
mL/min).

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment

71

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Mechanism of Action: Mirvetixumab soravtransine-gynx is an antibody-drug conjugate (ADC)
containing a chimeric IgG1 monoclonal antibody targeting folate receptor alpha (FRα) and a
cytotoxic payload (DM4), which is a microtubule inhibitor, attached to the antibody via a
cleavable linker. Upon release from the antibody, DM4 disrupts the microtubule network within
the cell, resulting in cell cycle arrest and apoptotic cell death.
Absorption: Mirvetixumab soravtransine-gynx is administered as an IV infusion. Peak
mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx concentrations were observed near the end of intravenous
infusion, while peak unconjugated DM4 concentrations were observed on the second day after
administration of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx, and the peak S-methyl-DM4 concentrations
were observed approximately 3 days after administration of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx.
Accumulation of the mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx, DM4, and S-methyl-DM4 was minimal
following repeat administration of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx.
Distribution: The mean (±SD) steady state volume of distribution of mirvetuximab soravtansinegynx was 2.63 (±2.98) L. Unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4 showed >99% plasma protein
binding. The blood:plasma ratio for DM4 in human blood was 0.58 – 1.85 with inversely
proportional to the concentration. The blood:plasma ratio for s-methyl DM4 in human blood
was 0.55 – 0.58.
Metabolism: Unconjugated DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 undergo metabolism by CYP3A4. In human
plasma, DM4 and S-methyl DM4 were identified as the main circulating metabolites, accounting
for approximately 0.4% and 1.4% of mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx AUCs, respectively.
Elimination: Non-clinical studies suggest that biliary excretion is the primary route of
elimination for DM4 and renal excretion exhibit minor involvement (0.7 %).
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The recommended dosage for mirvetuximab soravtansine is 6 mg/kg via IV Q3W based on
AIBW. This dosing regimen showed reasonable efficacy (ORR of 31.7% [95% CI: 22.9, 41.6]) with
a generally acceptable tolerability profile in the intended patient population. However, there
are significant concerns regarding the high risk of ocular toxicity at the 6 mg/kg Q3W AIBW
dosing regimen.
The FDA notes that among 106 patients who received treatment with ELAHERE at 6 mg/kg
AIBW Q3W in the pivotal study IMGN853-0417 with a median follow-up of 11.3 months, vision
impairment (any grade) occurred in 54% of patients, including 8% Grade 3 with no Grade 4

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

72

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
events reported. Thirty seven percent of patients had keratopathy (any grade) with 8% of
patients had Grade 3 and 1 patient with Grade 4 events reported.
Nearly 24% (25/106) of the patients with ocular AEs required at least one dose
interruption/reduction/discontinuation. Nineteen (19%) of patients required at least one dose
reduction due to blurred vision and 13% of the patients required at least one dose-reduction
due to keratopathy. Additional details about the safety of mirvetuximab soravtansine are
provided in 8.2.
Due to lack of robust dose finding data and very high rate of ocular AEs, a PMR will be issued in
order to identify an optimal dosage schedule of mirvetuximab soravtansine.
Therapeutic Individualization
No dose adjustments are proposed based on intrinsic or extrinsic factors. Based on population
PK analyses, mirvetuximab soravtansine PK was not affected by mild or moderate renal
impairment and mild hepatic impairment. Of note, only one patient with moderate hepatic
impairment was included in the analysis. There were no patients with either severe renal
impairment or severe hepatic impairment. In addition, intrinsic factors such as age and AIBW
were identified to be significant covariates in population PK analysis. However, the influence of
these covariates on the PK of mirvetuximab soravtansine was < ±20%. Nevertheless, AIBW was
found to be a significant covariate on IMGN853 exposure and therefore, the recommended
dosage is based on AIBW to lower the inter-subject variability. No dosage modifications for any
other covariates was warranted. Dedicated drug-drug interactions (DDI) studies were not
conducted with mirvetuximab soravtansine. In vitro studies suggest that mirvetuximab
soravtansine may exhibit potential DDIs.
There were insufficient number of non-white patients (91.7% White [n = 498], 2.2% Black [n =
12], 2.8% Asian [n = 15], 0.4% American Indian [n = 2], 2.9% Other or Not reported [n = 18]) in
the clinical studies included in the application. The Applicant did not assess the impact of race
and ethnicity in the population PK analysis. As outlined in the section below (clinical), a PMC
will be issued to conduct an integrated analysis containing data from clinical trials and other
data sources such as post-marketing reports, real-world evidence and other sources to further
characterize the safety and efficacy of mirvetuximab soravtansine in patients from racial and
ethnic minority groups.
Outstanding Issues
FDA will issue two PMRs to address the outstanding issues based on the review of the clinical
data submitted by the Applicant.
• There was a limited dose-exploration with mirvetuximab soravtansine in the doseescalation study (Study IMGN853-0401). The applicant selected the dose of 6 mg/kg
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

73

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
AIBW Q3W for the pivotal efficacy study (IMGN853-0417). A significantly higher rate of
ocular AEs was reported among all the patients treated at this dosage. Since the dose
escalation study enrolled very few patients at dosage schedules other than 6 mg/kg
Q3W, robust dose or exposure-response data were not available to enable optimal dose
selection. Based on available data, it is difficult to determine whether 6 mg/kg AIBW
Q3W of mirvetuximab soravtansine provides the optimal risk-benefit profile.
The limited clinical data obtained from the dose-escalation study suggest that
alternative dosing schedules; specifically, a fractionated dosing regimen where smaller
doses are administered more frequently (i.e., 3X QW doses over 4 weeks), could
possibly lower the incidence of ocular adverse events. Given the high rates of ocular
toxicity observed with the 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W dose, and limited data available at
alternative dosing regimens, additional clinical safety and efficacy information for
mirvetuximab soravtansine is needed to determine the optimal dosage that will
minimize the serious risk of ocular toxicity without a clinically significant impact on
efficacy. Therefore, the applicant will be asked to conduct a postmarketing dose finding
study to assess the safety and efficacy of alternative dose schedules of mirvetuximab
soravtansine.
•

The clinical pharmacology portions of the application contains data from only a limited
number of patients with moderate (n = 1) or severe hepatic impairment. Based on
nonclinical data, unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4 are CYP3A substrates. Therefore,
hepatic metabolism and/or excretion is expected to play a major role in the elimination
of unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4. Given that the hepatic impairment may
increase the exposure of unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4 and thereby increase
the risk of drug toxicity, additional information is required to determine appropriate and
safe dosing of mirvetuximab soravtansine in patients with moderate hepatic
impairment.

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
PHARMACOLOGY
Structure
Mirvetuximab soravtansine consists of an anti-FRα monoclonal antibody
(immunoglobulin G1 [IgG1]) attached via a cleavable linker to the cytotoxic
maytansinoid payload, DM4. Maytansinoids are anti-mitotic agents that
inhibit tubulin polymerization and microtubule assembly, resulting in cell
cycle arrest and apoptosis. Mirvetuximab soravtansine contains an average of
3.4 DM4 molecules bound to the antibody. The payload is known to form the
active metabolite, S-methyl-DM4.
Mechanism of
The anticancer activity mirvetuximab soravtansine is due to the binding of the
Action
ADC to FRα expressing cells, followed by internalization of the ADC. Once
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

74

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
inside, the cytotoxic small molecule, DM4, is released via proteolytic cleavage.
DM4 disrupts the microtubule network within the cell, resulting in cell cycle
arrest and apoptotic cell death.
QT Prolongation In the dose-escalation/expansion phase 1 study (IMGN853-0401), no large
changes in mean QTc intervals (>20 msec) were noted at the recommended
dosage of mirvetuximab soravtansine.
Increases in QTcF of > 500 msec were not observed in the mirvetuximab
soravtansine arm. Central tendency analysis of the QTcF interval changes
from baseline showed that the highest mean (upper bound of the 2-sided
90% CI) for QTcF was 8.1 (4.1, 13.0) msec, which was observed at cycle 3/Day
1/end of infusion in the Expansion cohort 3 of mirvetuximab soravtansine
arm.
GENERAL INFORMATION
Bioanalysis
All Patients (Studies IMGN853-0401, IMGN853-0403, and IMGN853-0417)
Plasma concentrations of mirvetuximab soravtansine and total antibody were
measured using a validated ELISA method.
A validated high performance liquid chromatography coupled to tandem mass
spectrometry (MS/MS) detection was developed for the quantitation of DM4
and S-methyl DM4 analytes.

Dose
Proportionality

Accumulation
Variability

Immunogenicity

Immunogenicity
An ECL-based and a cell-based assay were used to detect ADA and nAb to
mirvetuximab soravtansine in human plasma.
Following IV infusion, the exposures (Cmax and AUC0-INF) of ADC and total Ab
showed dose-proportional increase at 3.3 to 7 mg/kg total BW. In the lower
dose range from 0.15 to 2 mg/kg TBW, the clearance decreased with
increasing dose, suggesting a saturable mechanism of elimination such as
target-mediated drug disposition.
There was minimal accumulation with repeated administrations.
Mean (%CV) Accumulation Ratios: ADC – 1.15 (22.4%); TAb – 1.41 (29.1%),
DM4 - 1.1 and s-methyl DM4 - 1.01.
Pharmacokinetic data obtained from the clinical studies show that
Mirvetuximab soravtansine exhibits low to moderate variability. The %CV for
AUCss and CMAX predicted with PopPK model were 22.6% and 17.7%,
respectively at the recommended dose.

DISTRIBUTION
Volume of
Distribution

6 mg/kg AIBW in EOC patients had at least 1 post-baseline positive sample for
anti-mirvetuximab soravtansine-gynx antibodies.
Of these, 28/423 patients (7%) had developed treatment-emergent ADA and
3/423 patients (<1%) had treatment-enhanced ADA.
Neutralizing antibodies were detected in 24/423 (6%) of patients.

Based on population PK analysis, the mean volume of distribution (%CV):
Cycle 1: 3.198 L (23.2%); Cycle 3: 3.172 L (32.3%) in Study IMGN853-0401.
The steady state Vdss = 2.63 (± 2.98) L. Based on the small Vd, distribution
may be primarily limited in plasma or in vascular rich tissues.
Plasma Protein
Unconjugated DM4 and s-methyl DM4 were highly bound to plasma proteins
Binding
(>99%).
Blood-to-Plasma In preclinical studies, mirvetuximab soravtansine exhibited limited
ratio
distribution to red blood cells in rats, monkeys, and humans. The blood-toplasma ratio in human blood was inversely proportional to concentration and
ranged between 0.58 – 1.85 for DM4. The blood-to-plasma ratio for s-methyl
DM4 was 0.55 – 0.58 in human blood.

METABOLISM
Primary
Nonclinical studies using human biomaterials showed that CYP3A4 is the
Metabolic
predominant CYP450 enzyme responsible for the biotransformation of DM4
Pathways
and S-methyl-DM4.
Transporter
• DM4 and S-methyl DM4 are substrates of the MDR1 but not inhibitors of
Substrate
MDR1.
• DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 were substrates of P-pg, but neither was an

DDI Potential
• DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 showed no direct inhibition on CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4/5.
• DM4 showed time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4, and S-methyl DM4
showed weak time-dependent inhibition of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9.
• DM4 and S-methyl-DM4 did not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.

EXCRETION
Primary
Excretion
Pathway

Dedicated DDI studies have not been conducted with mirvetuximab
soravtansine.
In preclinical species, feces, via biliary elimination, is the main route of
excretion following IV doses of mirvetuximab soravtansine. Urinary excretion
accounting for approximately 0.7% of the dose. No mass-balance studies
were conducted in human subjects.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
Yes, the clinical pharmacology data provide supportive evidence of effectiveness. The applicant
submitted results of three key studies to support the proposed BLA Table 6. These included a
Phase 1 dose-escalation/expansion trial (Study IMGN853-0401), a Phase 3 randomized study
(Study IMGN853-0403), and a pivotal Phase 3 single-arm, open-label study (Study IMGN8530417). Results of the dose-selection and the pivotal studies are discussed below.

Dosing Regimen Selection:
To support the proposed dosing regimen of 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W, the applicant rationalized
based on the risk:benefit assessment.
As shown in the Table 6 above, Study IMGN853-0401 evaluated several dose levels
administered in a Q3W schedule. In addition, a smaller cohort of the patients were also treated
with modified weekly dosing schedule (3 weekly doses over 4 weeks). However, there was
limited clinical data collected at dose-levels and/or schedules other than the recommended
dosage of 6 mg/kg AIBW Q3W.
Per the Applicant, based upon the totality of the safety, anti-tumor activity data, and PK, 6
mg/kg AIBW IV Q3W was declared the MTD/RP2D for the expansion cohorts. The use of the
AIBW dosing at 6 mg/kg ensured maximum dose intensity with a tolerable safety profile. As

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

77

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {Biologics License Application (BLA) 761310}
{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
treatment. The primary endpoint was ORR. The observed ORR rate in the pivotal study was
32.4% (95% CI: 23.6, 42.2 %) including 5 CR (4.8%) and 29 PR (27.6%).
Exposure-response relationship
Exposure-response (E-R) analyses for safety and efficacy were conducted using data collected
from the pivotal trial (Study IMGN853-0417) at a dose of 6 mg/kg (n = 106) and the supportive
phase 1 (Study IMGN853-0401) and phase 3 trial (Study IMGN853-0403) at doses ranging from
0.15 mg/kg to 7 mg/kg.
Due to the differences in study design, the efficacy endpoints were analyzed in two separate
data pools, one for the pivotal study (IMGN853-0417, N = 97), and the other for the supporting
studies IMGN853-0401 (patients meeting the IMGN853-0403 inclusion criteria, N = 36) and
IMGN853-0403 (N = 242) combined. The safety endpoints were analyzed with data from the
three studies pooled together (N = 542) and a summary is shown in Table 9.

The effect of severe renal impairment on the PK of mirvetuximab soravtansine, unconjugated

Hepatic impairment
No dose modification is recommended in patients with mild hepatic impairment. No formal
clinical trial has been conducted to assess the effect of hepatic function on the PK of
mirvetuximab soravtansine. The effect of mild hepatic impairment on mirvetuximab
soravtansine, free DM4 or s-methyl DM4 exposure was assessed using the population PK
model. The population PK analysis included patients with mild (n=71) hepatic impairment, in
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5076613

{Mirvetuximab soravtansine}
addition to patients with normal (n=386) hepatic function. Model-predicted Cycle 1 ADC, DM4
or s-methyl DM4 exposures in patients with mild hepatic impairment were similar to patients
with normal hepatic function (Figure 6). There was only 1 patient with moderate HI and 21
patients whose hepatic impairment status was missing. Given the limited number of patients
with moderate and severe HI, the exposures in these patients are unknown.
